  • A manifest — a JSON document listing the config and the layers. Its own digest is the image's digest, the sha256:... you see. A manifest list (or image index) maps architectures to manifests, which is how one tag serves both linux/amd64 and linux/arm64 (Node 0).

A tag like node:20-alpine is a mutable pointer to a digest. Digests are immutable and tags are not, which is why production deployments should reference image@sha256:... and why imagePullPolicy: Always exists. Pulling is plain HTTPS against a registry API: fetch the manifest, then fetch each layer the local store does not already have.

What docker run does, step by step

Every step below is a node in this document. That is the point of reading it.

  1. Resolve and pull. The client asks the daemon; the daemon asks the registry for the manifest, selects the right architecture, and downloads missing layers. Each is verified against its digest and unpacked into a directory in the local store.
  2. Prepare the filesystem. Create a writable upper directory and assemble an overlayfs mount with the image's layers as lower directories (Node 24). Also prepare the mount specifications for any volumes and bind mounts (Node 21).
  3. Create the cgroup. Make a directory under /sys/fs/cgroup and write cpu.max, cpu.weight, memory.max, pids.max (Node 27).
  4. Create the process with new namespaces. clone() with CLONE_NEWPID | CLONE_NEWNS | CLONE_NEWNET | CLONE_NEWUTS | CLONE_NEWIPC | CLONE_NEWCGROUP, plus CLONE_NEWUSER if rootless (Nodes 14, 26). The child is PID 1 in its new PID namespace.
  5. Wire the network. Create a veth pair; move one end into the new network namespace; attach the other to the bridge; assign an address; set routes; install NAT rules for published ports (Nodes 26, 32).
  6. Set the root. Inside the child: mount the overlay, then pivot_root to it, then unmount the old root — so the host's filesystem is no longer reachable at all, which is the difference between pivot_root and the escapable chroot.
  7. Mount the pseudo-filesystems. A fresh /proc (which now reflects the new PID namespace), /sys, a tmpfs on /dev with a minimal device set, /dev/pts, and /dev/shm (Nodes 21, 24).
  8. Set the hostname in the new UTS namespace.
  9. Apply the security posture. Set the UID and GID (Node 25); drop capabilities to the retained set; set no_new_privs; install the seccomp filter (Node 4); apply the AppArmor or SELinux label.
  10. Set the environment and working directory, and set up the standard descriptors — pipes back to the daemon for logging, or a pseudo-terminal for -it (Node 22).
  11. execve() the entrypoint (Node 35). Your process is now running.

Eleven steps, no new mechanism, all of it kernel features from 2002–2016 that existed for years before anyone packaged them together.

The layers of tooling above it

The vocabulary confuses people and the structure is simple:

  • runc — the low-level runtime. Reads an OCI bundle (a directory with a root filesystem and a config.json) and performs the eleven steps. It is essentially the reference implementation of the OCI runtime specification, and you can run it by hand.
  • containerd — the daemon that manages images, storage, networking and lifecycle, and calls runc to actually start things. This is what Kubernetes talks to, through the CRI (Container Runtime Interface).
  • The shim — one small process per container, which is runc's parent. It exists so that containerd can be restarted or upgraded without killing every running container, and so someone stays around to collect the exit status (Node 5's zombie problem).
  • Docker — a developer-facing layer over containerd: the CLI, the build system, compose, and the image-building experience. Kubernetes removed its Docker-specific support in 1.24 (2022) because containerd was underneath all along; images built by Docker are OCI images and continued to work, which is why "Kubernetes is dropping Docker" caused far more alarm than it warranted.
  • Podman — daemonless and rootless by default, using the user namespace from Node 26. No background daemon means containers are children of your shell and integrate with systemd naturally.
  • BuildKit — the modern builder: it constructs a dependency graph of the build, executes independent steps in parallel, supports cache mounts (RUN --mount=type=cache, which makes a persistent npm or apt cache across builds) and secret mounts (--mount=type=secret, which never lands in a layer — the fix for Node 24's credential-leak problem).

Where this is in your stack

Everything you know about Dockerfiles now has a mechanism behind it. Multi-stage builds work because only the final stage's layers become the image, so the compiler and the source never ship. COPY --from=builder /app/dist ./dist copies between overlay trees. .dockerignore shrinks the tree walked in step 1's build-time equivalent. Ordering instructions from least to most volatile is layer-cache management (Node 24). A distroless or FROM scratch image works because a statically linked binary needs only the kernel's stable syscall ABI (Node 4) — and it has no shell, so docker exec sh fails and you need kubectl debug's ephemeral containers, which join the target's namespaces with setns (Node 26).

Debugging a container from the host is namespace entry. nsenter -t <host-pid> -n -p -m puts a shell into a container's network, PID and mount namespaces without the container needing any tooling of its own — invaluable for a distroless image. docker inspect --format '{{.State.Pid}}' gets the host PID. ctr and crictl talk to containerd directly when the higher layers are the problem.

The security properties, stated honestly. A container's isolation rests entirely on kernel correctness, because there is one kernel and every container calls into it directly. A kernel privilege-escalation bug reachable from a container is a host compromise. That is a real and different risk profile from a virtual machine, and it is why untrusted multi-tenant workloads use gVisor (Node 4), Kata Containers, or Firecracker microVMs (Node 29) rather than plain containers. The mitigations that matter, in rough order of value: do not run as root; drop all capabilities; keep seccomp enabled; use a read-only root filesystem; and use a user namespace.

What you can do at this point: state exactly what a container is, describe an OCI image's three parts and why digests matter, walk the eleven steps of docker run naming the mechanism behind each, place runc/containerd/shim/Docker/Podman/BuildKit correctly, debug a container by entering its namespaces, and state its security properties honestly.

What's still broken: one kernel, shared. Every container on a machine trusts the same code, and a bug in it is a bug for all of them. Some workloads cannot accept that, and the answer predates containers by forty years.

Node 29 — virtual machines, and where containers are not enough

The limitation: containers share one kernel, so a kernel bug is a shared vulnerability, a kernel panic is a shared outage, and a container cannot run a different operating system.

The idea, and its age

Virtualisation is older than Unix. IBM's CP-40 and CP-67, from 1967, gave each user a complete simulated System/360 — and VM/370 (1972) made it a product that is still sold today as z/VM. Time-sharing and virtualisation were competing answers to the same 1960s question of how to share an expensive machine, and the answers differ in where the boundary sits: time-sharing shares one operating system among many users, virtualisation gives each user an entire machine.

A hypervisor (or virtual machine monitor) presents each guest with what looks like real hardware: a processor, memory, disks, network cards, an interrupt controller. The guest runs an unmodified operating system, which boots, installs its own interrupt table, manages its own page tables, and believes it owns the machine.

Two kinds. Type 1 runs directly on the hardware — VMware ESXi, Xen, Hyper-V, and KVM in practice. Type 2 runs as an application on a host OS — VirtualBox, VMware Workstation, QEMU standalone. The line blurs: KVM is a Linux kernel module, so Linux itself becomes the hypervisor, which is arguably both.

Why it was hard, and the instruction that fixed it

Gerald Popek and Robert Goldberg established in 1974 the requirement for efficient virtualisation: every instruction that could reveal or change privileged state must trap when executed unprivileged, so the hypervisor can intercept it.

x86 failed that test. Seventeen instructions behaved differently in user mode instead of trapping — popf, for instance, would silently ignore attempts to change the interrupt flag rather than faulting. So a guest kernel running unprivileged would get wrong answers with no way for the hypervisor to notice. Three responses:

  • Binary translation — VMware's approach from 1999, and a genuine feat: scan the guest's kernel code as it executes and rewrite the problematic instructions on the fly. It worked, it was complicated, and it cost performance.
  • Paravirtualisation — Xen's approach (2003): modify the guest kernel to call the hypervisor explicitly instead of executing privileged instructions. Fast, and it requires a guest that knows it is virtualised.
  • Hardware virtualisation — Intel VT-x (2005) and AMD-V added a new mode beneath ring 0, informally "ring −1" (Node 3). A guest kernel runs genuinely in ring 0 and the hypervisor sits below it, with hardware trapping the operations that need interception. This made virtualisation fast and straightforward, and it is why the cloud became economically possible in the years after 2006.

Memory needed the same treatment. A guest maintains page tables mapping guest-virtual to guest-physical addresses, and the hypervisor must map guest-physical to host-physical — two levels. Software did this with shadow page tables, which were expensive to maintain. Hardware added second-level address translation (Intel EPT, AMD NPT), so the MMU walks both levels itself. Devices needed it too: IOMMU (VT-d) translates the addresses a device uses for DMA (Node 2), which is what makes it safe to hand a physical network card or GPU directly to a guest.

Virtio solved the device problem the other way: rather than emulating a real network card register by register — slow and pointless, since the guest knows it is virtualised — define a paravirtual device with a shared ring-buffer interface. Every Linux guest has virtio drivers built in, which is why cloud images boot on any hypervisor and why virtio-net and virtio-blk are what your cloud instance actually uses.

Containers and virtual machines, compared honestly

ContainerVirtual machine
Kernelshared with the hostits own
Isolation boundarykernel correctnesshardware-enforced, much smaller attack surface
Start timemilliseconds — it is a clone()seconds — a full boot (Node 36)
Overhead per instancea processa kernel plus its memory
Densityhundreds per hosttens
Guest OSmust match the host kernelanything
Live migrationessentially noyes, mature

The middle ground is where the interesting work has happened. Firecracker (AWS, 2018, written in Rust) is a minimal virtual machine monitor built on KVM that emulates almost nothing — five devices — and boots a microVM in about 125 milliseconds with roughly 5 MB of overhead. It is what runs AWS Lambda and Fargate, and it exists because AWS needed hardware-level isolation between customers' functions with container-like start times. Kata Containers presents an OCI runtime interface and transparently runs each container inside a microVM. gVisor (Node 4) takes the opposite route, implementing the Linux syscall interface in a user-space process so the host kernel is barely exposed.

The genuine conclusion is that containers and virtual machines are not competitors; the standard production shape is containers inside virtual machines. Your Kubernetes nodes are EC2 instances. The virtual machine boundary separates tenants; the container boundary separates your own workloads and gives you packaging and density. Anyone presenting it as a choice has usually not run multi-tenant infrastructure.

Where this is in your stack

Docker Desktop on macOS and Windows runs a Linux virtual machine, and this explains a great deal of daily friction. Containers are a Linux kernel feature (Node 26); macOS and Windows do not have those features, so there is a Linux VM — via Apple's Virtualization.framework or WSL2's Hyper-V-based VM — and your containers run inside it. Consequences: the "Docker is using 4 GB" you see is the VM's allocation, not your containers'; bind mounts cross a filesystem boundary and are slow (Node 24); --network host means the VM's network, not your Mac's, so it does not do what you expect; and containers cannot see your Mac's localhost except through the special host.docker.internal name. Every one of those is the VM boundary showing.

WSL2 is a virtual machine and WSL1 was not, and the difference is instructive. WSL1 translated Linux system calls into Windows NT kernel calls — a compatibility layer, in the spirit of Node 4's observation that the syscall interface is Linux. It was fast for Windows filesystem access and incomplete, and it could not run Docker. WSL2 runs a real Linux kernel in a lightweight VM, so it is fully compatible, and its filesystem story inverts: files under the Linux root are fast, files under /mnt/c cross the boundary and are slow. Keeping a project inside the WSL2 filesystem rather than under /mnt/c is often a five-to-tenfold difference on a Node build, and the reason is one line of this node.

Cloud instance types are this node's vocabulary. A "vCPU" is usually one hyperthread of a physical core, so two vCPUs is one core's worth of execution resources shared by two threads. Burstable instances (AWS T-family) accrue CPU credits while idle and are throttled to a baseline once they run out — the same failure shape as Node 23's EBS burst credits, and the reason a small instance that was fine for a week suddenly is not. Bare metal instances exist for workloads that cannot accept the hypervisor's overhead or that need to run their own hypervisor. And "noisy neighbours" is contention for shared physical resources — memory bandwidth, the L3 cache, the network — that no amount of vCPU allocation prevents.

Serverless is microVMs with a scheduler. A Lambda invocation runs inside a Firecracker microVM; a cold start is booting that microVM plus initialising your runtime plus demand-paging your code (Node 13). Understanding that tells you exactly which optimisations work: smaller deployment packages help because there is less to fault in; provisioned concurrency keeps the microVM warm; SnapStart snapshots the initialised state so the boot is replaced by a restore; and choosing more memory also gives more CPU, because Lambda allocates them together — which is why a CPU-bound function can be cheaper at a higher memory setting.

What you can do at this point: explain what a hypervisor is and why x86 was hard to virtualise, name the three historical solutions and what hardware support changed, compare containers and VMs on the axes that matter and say why the usual answer is both, explain what Firecracker and gVisor and Kata are for, explain Docker Desktop's and WSL2's architecture and the performance consequences, and explain a serverless cold start mechanically.

What's still broken: processes are now isolated in every dimension, which leaves the opposite problem. They need to talk — to each other, to the outside world, and to receive instructions like "shut down cleanly". None of that has been built, and the first piece of it is the reason your container ignores docker stop.

PART VII — TALKING: SIGNALS, IPC, AND THE NETWORK

Three nodes. How a process is told something happened, how two processes exchange data, and what the kernel does with a packet between the wire and your socket. By the end, graceful shutdown, EADDRINUSE, ECONNRESET, connection pooling, and a Kubernetes rolling update all have exact mechanical explanations.

Node 30 — signals, and why your container ignores docker stop

The limitation: a process is isolated and running, and nothing can tell it that something happened — that a user pressed Ctrl-C, that its child exited, that the system is shutting down.

What a signal is

A signal is a software interrupt delivered to a process: a small integer, and nothing else. No payload, no arguments, no return value. It is the mechanism from Node 2, applied one level up — asynchronous delivery that interrupts whatever the process was doing.

Delivery works like this. The kernel sets a bit in the target's pending-signal mask in its task_struct. When that process is next about to return to user mode — from a syscall, an interrupt, or a context switch — the kernel checks for pending signals. For each one it either applies the default action, or, if a handler is installed, arranges the stack so that the process resumes inside the handler and returns to where it was afterwards. So a signal is delivered when the process next runs, not at the instant it is sent, which is why signalling a stopped or uninterruptibly-sleeping process appears to do nothing (Node 5's D state).

The ones you need

Signal№DefaultWhat it means
SIGHUP1terminateThe terminal went away. Daemons reuse it as "reload your config".
SIGINT2terminateCtrl-C.
SIGQUIT3terminate + coreCtrl-\. In the JVM, dumps all thread stacks.
SIGKILL9terminateCannot be caught, blocked, or ignored. The kernel destroys the process.
SIGSEGV11terminate + coreInvalid memory access (Node 3).
SIGPIPE13terminateWrote to a pipe or socket with no reader (Node 22).
SIGTERM15terminatePlease shut down. The polite request, and the default of kill.
SIGCHLD17ignoreA child changed state. This is how you know to reap (Node 5).
SIGSTOP/SIGCONT19/18stop/resumeCtrl-Z and fg. SIGSTOP also cannot be caught.
SIGUSR1/SIGUSR210/12terminateReserved for the application to define.

The number matters because of exit codes. A process killed by signal N reports 128 + N. 137 is SIGKILL, 143 is SIGTERM, 139 is SIGSEGV, 130 is SIGINT. You can now decode any of them without looking anything up. [EXPLAIN]

The three restrictions that cause real bugs

One: SIGKILL and SIGSTOP cannot be handled. Deliberately — there must be a way to stop a process that refuses to cooperate. So there is no such thing as cleanup on SIGKILL: no flush, no close, no final log line. Every graceful-shutdown design must therefore happen on SIGTERM, and the OOM killer's use of SIGKILL (Node 13) is why an OOM kill gives you nothing.

Two: what a handler may do is severely restricted. A handler runs at an arbitrary point in the program — possibly in the middle of malloc, holding the allocator's lock. Calling malloc from the handler then deadlocks against the interrupted code. POSIX defines a short list of async-signal-safe functions that may be called; printf and malloc are not on it. The standard technique is the self-pipe trick: the handler writes one byte to a pipe and returns, and the main loop reads the pipe as an ordinary event. Linux later added signalfd, which turns signals into a readable descriptor directly. libuv uses exactly this, which is why in Node your process.on('SIGTERM') callback is ordinary JavaScript with no restrictions at all — by the time it runs, it is a normal event-loop callback, not a signal handler.

Three: standard signals do not queue. The pending set is a bitmask, so ten SIGCHLDs arriving while one is blocked result in one pending bit. This is why a SIGCHLD handler must reap in a loop — while (waitpid(-1, &st, WNOHANG) > 0); — rather than reaping once. Real-time signals (SIGRTMIN..SIGRTMAX) do queue and carry a small value, and are rarely used.

PID 1 is special, and this is the node's payoff

Two kernel behaviours attach specifically to PID 1 in its PID namespace:

Signals with default actions are not delivered to it. The kernel refuses, on the grounds that killing init would take down the system. So if PID 1 has not installed a handler for SIGTERM, the signal is silently discarded.

It inherits orphans and is expected to reap them (Node 5).

Now put that together with Node 28: your container's entrypoint is PID 1. Therefore:

  • docker stop sends SIGTERM, waits ten seconds, then sends SIGKILL. A Node process as PID 1 with no SIGTERM handler ignores the first signal entirely and is killed at ten seconds, dropping every in-flight request. The container appears to "take ten seconds to stop", and the cause is not slowness — it is that the polite request was discarded.
  • If your entrypoint is a shell (CMD npm start, which runs sh -c "npm start"), the shell is PID 1 and does not forward signals to its child. Your Node process never even hears about the shutdown. This is what ENTRYPOINT ["node", "server.js"] — exec form, not shell form — fixes, and it is the most common Dockerfile defect in the wild.
  • If your app spawns subprocesses, nothing reaps them, and zombies accumulate. docker run --init inserts tini as PID 1 to forward signals and reap; Kubernetes' shareProcessNamespace makes the pause container do it.

Where this is in your stack

Correct graceful shutdown in Node, with every line justified:

const server = app.listen(3000);
let shuttingDown = false;

async function shutdown(signal) {
  if (shuttingDown) return;          // a second Ctrl-C shouldn't re-enter
  shuttingDown = true;

  // 1. Fail readiness first, so the load balancer stops sending new work
  //    BEFORE we stop accepting it. Ordering matters — see below.
  healthy = false;
  await sleep(5000);                 // let the LB notice (its probe period)

  // 2. Stop accepting new connections; existing ones finish.
  server.close(() => console.log('http closed'));
  server.closeIdleConnections?.();   // Node 18.2+: drop keep-alive idlers

  // 3. Drain the rest: queue workers, then the DB pool.
  await worker.close();
  await pool.end();

  // 4. A hard deadline, below the orchestrator's grace period, so we
  //    always exit on our own terms rather than being SIGKILLed.
  setTimeout(() => process.exit(1), 10000).unref();
}

process.on('SIGTERM', () => shutdown('SIGTERM'));
process.on('SIGINT',  () => shutdown('SIGINT'));

The ordering in step 1 is the part people get wrong, and it is a race between two systems. In Kubernetes, when a pod is deleted, two things happen concurrently: the kubelet sends SIGTERM, and the endpoints controller removes the pod from the Service's endpoint list. The second propagates through kube-proxy or the service mesh on every node, which takes time. If you stop accepting connections the instant you get SIGTERM, traffic still being routed to you is refused — visible to users as a burst of 502s during every deploy. Delaying the actual shutdown by a few seconds (or using a preStop hook with a sleep) lets the removal propagate first. This is one of the most common causes of "we get errors during every rolling update", and it is not a bug in Kubernetes; it is a distributed-systems race that your process is expected to absorb.

terminationGracePeriodSeconds is the SIGKILL deadline — 30 seconds by default. Your own hard timeout must be below it, or you never exit cleanly. If your requests can legitimately take longer, raise the grace period rather than hoping.

SIGPIPE and EPIPE. Node ignores SIGPIPE and surfaces the condition as an EPIPE error on the stream instead — which is why a write to a socket the client already closed emits an 'error' event, and why an unhandled 'error' listener on a stream crashes the process. A client that navigates away mid-response is the common cause, and it is normal traffic rather than a fault.

SIGHUP as reload. nginx, Postgres, and most daemons re-read their configuration on SIGHUP rather than restarting — this is convention, not kernel behaviour, and it is why nginx -s reload keeps connections alive. SIGUSR1 and SIGUSR2 are similarly conventional: SIGUSR1 on a Node process opens the debugger port, which is a genuinely useful way to attach a profiler to a running production process.

kill does not mean kill. The command sends a signal, defaulting to SIGTERM. kill -9 is SIGKILL. Reaching for -9 first is a habit worth losing: it skips every cleanup path the program has, and if SIGTERM did not work, the reason is worth knowing — usually a missing handler, a shell as PID 1, or a D-state hang that -9 will not fix either.

What you can do at this point: explain what a signal is and how delivery works, name the ones that matter and derive exit codes from them, explain why SIGKILL cannot be handled and what that implies for cleanup, explain handler restrictions and how libuv sidesteps them, explain PID 1's two special behaviours and diagnose the "container ignores docker stop" family of bugs, and write a correct graceful shutdown including the endpoint-removal race.

What's still broken: a signal carries one integer. Processes need to exchange actual data.

Node 31 — inter-process communication

The limitation: a signal carries one integer, and isolated processes need to exchange data.

Every mechanism below trades the same three things: how fast it is, whether the parties must be related, and whether it works across machines. Knowing the trade is more useful than memorising the list.

The mechanisms

Pipes (Node 22) — a kernel buffer, unidirectional, between related processes. Built-in backpressure. A named pipe or FIFO (mkfifo) is the same object given a filesystem name, so unrelated processes can use it. Both copy data through the kernel: one copy in, one copy out.

Unix domain sockets — the same API as network sockets (socket, bind, listen, accept) with a filesystem path instead of an address, and no networking underneath. Bidirectional, supporting both stream and datagram semantics, and typically two to three times faster than a TCP connection over loopback because the entire protocol stack is skipped. Two abilities make them special:

  • They can pass file descriptors. SCM_RIGHTS in a control message transfers an open descriptor to another process, where it lands as a new number in that process's table referring to the same open file description (Node 22). This is how Node's cluster module hands a listening socket to workers, and how systemd socket activation works.
  • They can pass verified credentials. SO_PEERCRED tells you the connecting process's real UID, GID and PID, checked by the kernel and unforgeable. This is how the Docker daemon authorises access via /var/run/docker.sock — which is also why mounting that socket into a container is equivalent to giving it root on the host, since anything that can talk to the daemon can start a privileged container.

Shared memory — the fastest possible, because there is no copy at all: the same physical frames appear in two address spaces (Node 10). POSIX shm_open plus mmap, or the older System V shmget, or simply mmap of a file with MAP_SHARED. The cost is that it provides no synchronisation whatsoever — you are back in Node 16's territory and must supply your own atomics or locks, and a shared-memory bug is a data race across a process boundary. Postgres's shared_buffers is a shared mapping, and its internal LWLocks are the synchronisation over it.

Message queues — kernel-held queues of discrete messages with priorities. POSIX (mq_open) or System V (msgget). Preserve message boundaries, which streams do not. Rarely used in modern application code, largely because user-space brokers are more capable.

Semaphores and futexes (Node 17) — synchronisation rather than data transfer, but shared between processes when placed in shared memory.

Network sockets — Node 32, and the only one that crosses machines.

The trade, stated as a decision

Copies through the kernel cost about the same as a system call plus a memory copy — fine for kilobytes, wasteful for megabytes. Shared memory costs nothing per byte and everything in complexity. Unix sockets are the right default for local IPC because they are as fast as anything that copies, use the same API as networking so code ports trivially, and carry descriptors and credentials. Reach for shared memory only when profiling proves the copy is the bottleneck, which is much rarer than people assume.

Where this is in your stack

Node's cluster IPC channel is a Unix socket pair. child.send(obj) serialises to JSON (or, since Node 8, the richer V8 serialisation for some types), writes to the socket, and the parent's 'message' event fires from an ordinary event-loop read. So messaging between cluster workers costs serialisation plus two copies plus two syscalls per message — cheap for coordination and completely wrong for a shared cache. The listening socket, by contrast, is passed once as a descriptor using SCM_RIGHTS, which is why every worker can accept on the same port without any of them binding it.

worker_threads is not IPC at all, and the contrast is instructive. Workers are threads in one process (Node 15), so their postMessage is an in-memory structured clone with no kernel involvement — no syscall, no socket, just a copy on the heap. transferList avoids even the copy. And SharedArrayBuffer is shared memory, with Atomics as the synchronisation, which is precisely this node's shared-memory row with all of its hazards.

nginx to your Node app over a Unix socket. proxy_pass http://unix:/run/app.sock; instead of http://127.0.0.1:3000 avoids the entire TCP stack: no three-way handshake, no ephemeral port allocation, no TIME_WAIT accumulation (Node 32), and lower latency. It also means the service is unreachable from the network by construction, with filesystem permissions as the access control. The same applies to Postgres — psql on the same host connects over /var/run/postgresql/.s.PGSQL.5432 by default, and switching an application from host=localhost to host=/var/run/postgresql is a real, free latency reduction for a high-query-rate service. It is also why pg_hba.conf distinguishes local from host lines, with peer authentication on the former using SO_PEERCRED.

/var/run/docker.sock, restated because it matters. The Docker daemon listens on a Unix socket and authorises by peer credentials. Mounting it into a container — common for CI runners and for tools that inspect containers — hands that container the ability to create privileged containers, mount the host filesystem, and read every secret on the machine. It is a full host compromise waiting to happen, and the alternatives are a rootless daemon, a socket proxy that whitelists specific API calls, or building images with a daemonless tool such as Kaniko or BuildKit's rootless mode.

Redis as an IPC broker across processes and machines. Once you have several Node processes — cluster workers, replicas in Kubernetes, serverless instances — nothing in this node works between them, because they may not share a machine. That is the mechanical reason Redis ends up in so many architectures: it is the shared mutable state that separate address spaces (Node 5) cannot have. Rate limiter counters, session stores, cache, pub/sub for websocket fan-out, and BullMQ's queues are all one problem — state that must be visible to processes with separate address spaces — solved by putting it in a process that everyone can reach over a socket. Recognising that unifies half of a typical backend architecture.

What you can do at this point: list the IPC mechanisms with their trade-offs and choose correctly, explain descriptor passing and why cluster needs it, explain peer credentials and why the Docker socket is dangerous, explain why worker_threads messaging is not IPC, and explain Redis's role in an architecture as a consequence of address-space isolation.

What's still broken: all of this is local. The moment two processes are on different machines, everything changes — packets are lost, reordered, duplicated and delayed, there is no shared kernel to arbitrate, and the connection abstraction that hides all of it is the most elaborate thing in the operating system.

Node 32 — the kernel network stack, from the wire to your socket

The limitation: everything so far is local. Between machines, packets are lost, reordered, duplicated and delayed, and no shared kernel arbitrates.

The socket

Introduced in 4.2BSD in 1983 by Bill Joy's group at Berkeley, the socket API deliberately reused the file descriptor (Node 22) so that network endpoints could be read and written like anything else — the "everything is a file" decision from Node 21 paying off again. socket() creates one; bind() attaches a local address and port; listen() marks it passive; accept() returns a new descriptor for one connection; connect() initiates one.

Note the shape of accept: the listening socket stays listening and each connection gets its own descriptor. A connection is identified by a four-tuple — source address, source port, destination address, destination port. That tuple is what the kernel hashes to route an incoming packet to the right socket, and it explains several limits later.

Establishing a connection, and the two queues

TCP's three-way handshake: the client sends SYN with its initial sequence number; the server replies SYN-ACK with its own; the client sends ACK. One full round trip before any data moves — and TLS adds one more (two in TLS 1.2), which is the entire reason connection reuse matters as much as it does.

The kernel keeps two queues, and conflating them is a common error:

  • The SYN queue holds half-open connections that have received a SYN and are awaiting the final ACK. Sized by net.ipv4.tcp_max_syn_backlog. Flooding it with SYNs from forged addresses is a SYN flood, and the defence — SYN cookies — is elegant: encode the connection state cryptographically into the sequence number itself so no state need be kept until the handshake completes.
  • The accept queue holds fully established connections waiting for your program to call accept(). Sized by the backlog argument to listen(), capped by net.core.somaxconn (512 on Linux by default, 4096 on recent kernels; Node passes 511 by default). When this queue is full, new connections are dropped — silently, from the client's point of view, appearing as a connection timeout or a reset.

That second queue is a real production signal. It fills when your process is not calling accept fast enough, which for a Node server means the event loop is blocked (Node 20). ss -lnt shows Recv-Q as the current accept-queue depth and Send-Q as its limit, and nstat -az TcpExtListenOverflows counts the drops. [USE] A rising ListenOverflows with healthy CPU is one of the cleanest possible diagnoses of a blocked loop.

Reliability, and flow, and congestion

TCP turns a lossy packet channel into an ordered byte stream using: sequence numbers on every byte; acknowledgements for what has been received (with selective acknowledgement, SACK, to report holes precisely); retransmission on timeout, where the timeout is derived from a smoothed round-trip-time estimate and doubles on each failure; and reordering tolerance, buffering out-of-order data until the gap fills.

Two separate limits govern how fast data flows, and people routinely confuse them:

  • Flow control protects the receiver. Every acknowledgement carries a receive window — how much buffer space remains — and the sender may not exceed it. A receiver that reads slowly shrinks the window to zero and the sender stops. This is TCP-level backpressure, and it is the same principle as the pipe in Node 22 and the stream in Node 19.
  • Congestion control protects the network. The sender maintains a congestion window it grows on success and shrinks on loss. Slow start begins with about ten packets and doubles each round trip — which is why the first ~14 KB of a response arrives in one round trip and larger responses take several, and why "keep your critical CSS under 14 KB" was ever meaningful advice. CUBIC is Linux's default: grow aggressively, halve on loss. BBR (Google, 2016) instead models the path's bandwidth and round-trip time directly rather than treating loss as the congestion signal, which performs far better on links that drop packets for reasons other than congestion — mobile networks, long-distance links — and is why enabling it on an edge server sometimes produces a startling improvement.

Bandwidth-delay product is the number that ties them together: a 100 ms round trip at 1 Gbps needs about 12 MB in flight to keep the pipe full. If the receive buffer is smaller than that, throughput is capped by latency regardless of available bandwidth. This is why net.ipv4.tcp_rmem and tcp_wmem autotuning exists and why long-distance transfers are slow until buffers are sized for them.

Closing, and TIME_WAIT

Closing is a four-way exchange (FIN, ACK, FIN, ACK) because each direction closes independently. The side that closes first then enters TIME_WAIT and stays there for twice the maximum segment lifetime — 60 seconds on Linux, not tunable without recompiling.

Two genuine reasons, both worth knowing: a delayed duplicate packet from this connection must not be delivered to a new connection reusing the same four-tuple; and if the final ACK is lost, the peer will retransmit its FIN and someone must be there to answer.

The consequence is that the side that closes first accumulates sockets in TIME_WAIT for a minute each, and since a client's source ports come from the ephemeral range (net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range, about 28,000 ports by default), a client opening more than roughly 470 connections per second to the same destination exhausts them and gets EADDRNOTAVAIL. Hence:

  • SO_REUSEADDR lets a server bind a port that has connections in TIME_WAIT. Without it, restarting a server fails with EADDRINUSE for a minute — which is why every server framework sets it, Node included.
  • SO_REUSEPORT (Linux 3.9, 2013) is different and more powerful: several processes may bind the same port, and the kernel load-balances incoming connections between them by hashing the four-tuple. This is an alternative to Node's cluster primary distributing descriptors, and it is what nginx workers use.
  • tcp_tw_reuse allows reusing TIME_WAIT sockets for new outbound connections. Note that tcp_tw_recycle, which older tuning guides recommend, was removed in Linux 4.12 because it broke badly behind NAT.
  • The real fix is not to open so many connections — keep-alive and pooling, below.

Where this is in your stack

Every network error you have seen, decoded:

  • ECONNREFUSED — the destination replied with RST: the host is reachable and nothing is listening on that port. Fast, and definitive. Usually the wrong port, or the service has not started.
  • ETIMEDOUT on connect — no reply at all. A firewall dropping silently, a wrong address, or a security group. Slow, because it waits out retransmissions.
  • ECONNRESET — the peer sent RST on an established connection: it crashed, its socket had unread data when it closed, an idle timeout expired at a load balancer, or a keep-alive connection was reused just as the server closed it. That last case is the common one in Node, and it is a race: your agent picks a pooled socket the server is simultaneously closing. Mitigations are to keep the client's idle timeout below the server's, and to retry idempotent requests on ECONNRESET.
  • EPIPE — you wrote to a connection the peer has closed (Node 30).
  • EADDRINUSE — the port is taken, or held in TIME_WAIT without SO_REUSEADDR.
  • EMFILE — descriptor limit (Node 19).

HTTP keep-alive is the direct answer to the handshake cost. A new HTTPS connection costs a TCP round trip plus a TLS round trip plus slow start; reusing one costs nothing. Node's http.Agent had keepAlive: false by default until Node 19, which meant every outbound request paid the full cost — a genuinely significant and widely unnoticed latency tax on service-to-service calls. Set keepAlive: true, set maxSockets deliberately, and prefer undici (Node's modern HTTP client, and the implementation behind global fetch), whose pooling and pipelining are considerably better. In AWS Lambda this is the single highest-value line of configuration for anything calling other services, because a function instance handles many invocations and without pooling every one re-handshakes.

A Kubernetes Service is packet rewriting, not a proxy. A ClusterIP has no process behind it. kube-proxy programs the kernel — historically with iptables DNAT rules, then IPVS for large clusters, and now increasingly eBPF via Cilium — so that a packet addressed to the Service IP has its destination rewritten to a randomly chosen pod IP by netfilter, in the kernel, on the sending node. Consequences that follow directly: load balancing is per connection, not per request, so a long-lived HTTP/2 or gRPC connection pins to one pod forever and traffic distributes badly — which is exactly why service meshes and gRPC clients need explicit load balancing. Also, the iptables ruleset grows with the number of endpoints, which is why very large clusters moved to IPVS and eBPF.

Connection tracking is a finite table and it fills up. NAT and stateful firewalling require the kernel to remember every connection in nf_conntrack. The table has a size (nf_conntrack_max), and when it is full new connections are dropped and the kernel logs nf_conntrack: table full, dropping packet. On a busy Kubernetes node with many short-lived connections this is a real and confusing outage — intermittent connection failures across every workload on the node, with all applications healthy. Diagnose with conntrack -C against nf_conntrack_max.

Head-of-line blocking, at three layers, which is the clearest way to see why HTTP evolved. HTTP/1.1 has it at the request layer: one response must complete before the next on that connection, which is why browsers open six connections per host. HTTP/2 multiplexes streams over one connection and removes it at the HTTP layer — but not at the TCP layer, because a single lost packet stalls every stream until it is retransmitted, since TCP must deliver bytes in order. HTTP/3 solves that by abandoning TCP entirely for QUIC over UDP, implementing reliability per-stream in user space, so one stream's loss does not block the others. It also merges the transport and TLS handshakes into one round trip. Every step of that evolution is a response to something in this node.

MTU and the mysteries it causes. Ethernet's payload limit is 1500 bytes, and TCP negotiates a maximum segment size accordingly. Overlay networks — VXLAN in Kubernetes, WireGuard, IPsec VPNs — add encapsulation headers, so the effective MTU inside is smaller. If a packet is too large and the "don't fragment" bit is set, a router should return an ICMP "fragmentation needed" message, and path MTU discovery adjusts. When a firewall blocks that ICMP message — which many do, in the name of security — you get a PMTU black hole: small requests succeed and large ones hang forever. The classic symptom is that curl of a health endpoint works and a POST with a large body times out, on the same connection path. It is one of the most maddening network bugs there is, and recognising the signature saves days.

DNS in Kubernetes is a latency source people miss. The default ndots: 5 in a pod's /etc/resolv.conf means any name with fewer than five dots is first tried against every search domain — so resolving api.example.com issues four or five failing queries before the right one, for both A and AAAA records. Fixes: a trailing dot to make the name absolute, an explicit dnsConfig with a lower ndots, or NodeLocal DNSCache. Also relevant from Node 19: dns.lookup uses the threadpool, so slow DNS blocks file I/O too.

What you can do at this point: explain the socket API and the four-tuple, explain both backlog queues and use accept-queue overflow to detect a blocked event loop, distinguish flow control from congestion control and explain slow start's effect on response times, explain TIME_WAIT and derive port exhaustion and SO_REUSEADDR/SO_REUSEPORT, decode every common network error code by mechanism, explain a Kubernetes Service as netfilter rules and why gRPC balances badly over it, and recognise conntrack exhaustion and PMTU black holes.

What's still broken: everything in Parts I to VII assumed a running program existed. Nothing has explained how a text file becomes machine code, how that code finds the library functions it calls, what execve does with the resulting file, or how the machine gets from powered-off to running any of it.

PART VIII — FROM SOURCE TO RUNNING, AND FROM POWER-ON TO PID 1

Five nodes. Everything so far assumed a running program existed. This part builds one: source to object to executable, how it finds the library code it calls, what execve does with the file, how the machine gets from powered-off to running init, and what init then does. It also contains the most productive analogy-free parallel in this document — a JavaScript bundler is a linker, and every one of its concepts has an exact counterpart here.

Node 33 — compiling, linking, and the ELF file

The limitation: programs have been assumed to exist as bytes in a file, with nothing said about how source text becomes those bytes.

The pipeline

Four stages, and they are separate programs, which is why each can fail with its own kind of error:

  1. Preprocessing — textual substitution: includes, macros, conditional compilation. Output is still source.
  2. Compiling — parse, type-check, optimise, and emit assembly for one translation unit. The compiler sees one file at a time and nothing else, which is the fact that makes stage 4 necessary.
  3. Assembling — turn assembly text into machine code (Node 0), producing an object file.
  4. Linking — combine object files into one executable.

What an object file contains, and why linking is a real problem

An object file holds machine code for its own functions, but the compiler could not know the addresses of anything defined elsewhere. So it emits placeholders and two tables describing them:

  • The symbol table — names this file defines (with offsets) and names it references but does not define (undefined symbols).
  • Relocations — a list of positions in the code that contain a placeholder, and instructions for how to patch each one once the real address is known.

The linker then does exactly two things. Symbol resolution: for every undefined symbol, find the object file that defines it. Relocation: assign final addresses to everything and patch every placeholder. [EXPLAIN]

Its two failure modes are the two ways that can go wrong, and both have exact counterparts in your daily work:

  • Undefined reference — nothing defines this symbol. A missing library, a typo, or a declaration with no definition.
  • Multiple definition — two objects define the same symbol and the linker cannot choose.

A static library (.a) is just an archive of object files with an index. The linker pulls out only the members that resolve something still undefined — which is why link order matters in C and why a library listed before the code that uses it silently contributes nothing.

ELF

The output format on Linux is ELF — Executable and Linkable Format, adopted from System V in the 1990s, replacing the older a.out. (macOS uses Mach-O; Windows uses PE/COFF. Same concepts, different containers.)

The structure worth knowing is that ELF has two views of the same bytes:

  • Section headers — the linker's view. Fine-grained: .text (code), .rodata (constants), .data (initialised globals), .bss (zero-initialised globals, which occupy no space in the file — only a size — which is why a program with a 100 MB zeroed array is a small file), .symtab, .debug_*.
  • Program headers — the loader's view. Coarse: a handful of segments, each of which is a region to mmap into memory with given permissions. This is the direct input to Node 11's address space layout: one read-execute segment for code, one read-only for constants, one read-write for data.

Useful tools: readelf -h (header — architecture, type, entry point), readelf -l (segments), nm (symbols), objdump -d (disassembly), file (a one-line summary including whether it is static or dynamic). [USE]

Two ELF types matter. ET_EXEC is a fixed-address executable. ET_DYN is position-independent — loadable at any address, which is what ASLR (Node 10) requires, and which is why essentially all modern binaries are built as position-independent executables and why file reports them, confusingly, as "shared object".

Where this is in your stack

A JavaScript bundler is a linker. The correspondence is exact rather than suggestive, and internalising it makes bundler behaviour predictable. [EXPLAIN]

LinkingBundling
object file (.o)module (one .js file after transform)
undefined symbolimport { x } from 'y'
defined symbolexport
symbol resolutionmodule resolution — walking node_modules, exports maps, aliases
undefined referenceModule not found / does not provide an export named
multiple definitiontwo copies of React — and the resulting hook errors
static library (.a)a package in node_modules
dead code elimination (--gc-sections)tree shaking
relocationrewriting import references to bundle-internal identifiers
stripping symbolsminification
DWARF debug infosource maps
shared library, loaded at run time (Node 34)code splitting and import()
link order affecting what gets pulled inside effects, and "sideEffects": false

Several bundler behaviours become obvious once you read that table. Tree shaking requires static imports for the same reason dead-code elimination requires static references: a linker cannot remove a function if something might look it up by name at run time, and a bundler cannot remove an export if the module might be accessed dynamically. That is why ES modules can be tree-shaken and CommonJS's require largely cannot — require(someVariable) is unresolvable at build time, exactly like a function pointer defeats a linker's reachability analysis. "sideEffects": false in a package.json is you telling the bundler that importing a module for its side effects alone never happens, which lets it drop unreferenced modules entirely — the same promise --gc-sections relies on. And two copies of React is a multiple-definition problem that the JavaScript module system, unlike a linker, does not report as an error: it silently gives you two module instances with two separate pieces of internal state, which is why hooks fail with an error about an invalid hook call rather than about duplication.

Native Node addons are this node exactly. A .node file is an ELF shared object compiled from C++ by node-gyp, linking against the Node and V8 headers. Hence: it is architecture-specific and platform-specific (Node 0), which is why npm install on your Mac produces artefacts a Linux container cannot load; it must match the ABI of the Node version, which is what NODE_MODULE_VERSION mismatch means, and what N-API was introduced to fix by providing a stable ABI across Node versions; and it needs a compiler toolchain at install time unless the package ships prebuilt binaries, which is what prebuild-install and node-pre-gyp do and why sharp installs instantly on common platforms and slowly on unusual ones.

The stages explain the errors. A syntax error is stage 2; a missing header is stage 1; undefined reference to ... is stage 4. In the TypeScript world the parallel is worth drawing carefully: tsc is stages 1–3 per file and there is no linker — type errors are compile-time and module resolution happens at run time or at bundle time. That is precisely why a project can type-check cleanly and fail at run time with a module-not-found error: the two checks are performed by different tools looking at different things, and tsc's paths being satisfied does not mean the runtime's resolver will agree.

What you can do at this point: describe the four stages and attribute an error to the right one, explain what an object file's symbol table and relocations are for and what a linker does, read an ELF file's two views, and map every bundler concept to its linker counterpart well enough to predict tree-shaking and duplicate-module behaviour.

What's still broken: the executable still contains unresolved references — to printf, to malloc, to every library function. Baking a copy of every library into every program means one security fix requires rebuilding everything on the machine, and a hundred programs carrying a hundred copies of the same code. The alternative introduces a whole new failure surface, and it is the reason an Alpine image sometimes will not run your binary.

Node 34 — dynamic linking, libc, and why Alpine sometimes breaks things

The limitation: static linking copies every library into every program, so one security fix means rebuilding everything, and a hundred programs carry a hundred copies of the same code.

Deferring the link to run time

Dynamic linking leaves references unresolved in the executable and records instead which libraries are needed. At run time, a program called the dynamic linker — /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2 on Linux, itself named in the executable's PT_INTERP segment — maps the required shared objects into the address space and performs relocation before the program's first instruction runs.

The benefits: one copy of libc in memory shared by every process, since its code pages are mapped from one set of physical frames (Node 10, Node 14); a security fix in a library takes effect for every program on the next start; and executables are far smaller.

The costs, all real: startup does work that used to be done at build time; the deployment environment must supply compatible libraries; and version mismatches become a run-time failure class rather than a build-time one.

The machinery: PLT and GOT

Worth knowing because it explains both lazy binding and a security mitigation you have configured without knowing.

A call to a library function does not jump to the function. It jumps to a stub in the Procedure Linkage Table, which reads an address from the Global Offset Table and jumps there. Initially each GOT entry points back into the linker, so the first call to a function triggers resolution — the linker finds the real address, writes it into the GOT, and jumps. Every subsequent call goes straight through. That is lazy binding: symbols are resolved on first use, so a program that never calls a function never pays to resolve it.

Because the GOT is written at run time it is a natural attack target — overwrite an entry and redirect a call. The mitigation is RELRO: resolve everything eagerly at startup and mark the GOT read-only. LD_BIND_NOW=1 or linking with -z now enables full RELRO, trading slightly slower startup for a closed attack surface, and it is standard on hardened builds.

Finding libraries, and the variables involved

The search order at run time: DT_RPATH in the binary (deprecated), then LD_LIBRARY_PATH from the environment, then DT_RUNPATH, then the cache built by ldconfig from /etc/ld.so.conf, then the default directories.

ldd <binary> lists what it needs and where each was found; not found lines are exactly the cause of error while loading shared libraries: libfoo.so.1: cannot open shared object file. [USE]

LD_PRELOAD is the interesting one: a list of libraries loaded before all others, so their symbols win. This lets you replace any library function in any program without recompiling it. Legitimate uses are common — swapping in jemalloc (Node 12) with a single environment variable, injecting tracing or fault injection, or making a program use a different DNS resolver. It is also a classic persistence technique for attackers, which is why it is ignored for setuid binaries.

Versioning, and the error you will meet

A shared library has a soname like libssl.so.3, where the number is an ABI version. Binaries record the soname, so incompatible versions can coexist. glibc goes further with symbol versioning, keeping multiple implementations of a function under versioned names (memcpy@GLIBC_2.2.5) so that old binaries keep old behaviour.

The practical consequence is an asymmetry worth internalising: glibc is backward compatible but not forward compatible. A binary built against glibc 2.31 runs on a system with 2.36. A binary built against 2.36 fails on 2.31 with version 'GLIBC_2.34' not found. So build on the oldest system you must support, which is why release pipelines pin an old base image, and why building in a modern container and running on an older host fails in this specific way.

musl versus glibc: what Alpine actually changes

Alpine Linux uses musl rather than glibc — a different, smaller, MIT-licensed C library. The image is small (about 5 MB base) and that is genuinely valuable. The differences are also genuine and worth knowing before choosing:

  • Binaries are not interchangeable. Anything compiled against glibc will not run, which is why prebuilt native modules often fail on Alpine and fall back to compiling from source — turning a fast install into a slow one, and requiring a toolchain in the image.
  • DNS resolution differs. musl has no NSS, so it ignores /etc/nsswitch.conf, and it queries A and AAAA records in parallel over the same socket. Some resolvers and middleboxes handle that badly, producing intermittent resolution failures that are very hard to reproduce. It also historically did not honour search domains the same way, which matters inside Kubernetes (Node 32).
  • musl's allocator is slower under multi-threaded allocation-heavy load. This is measurable in Node workloads, and it was significant enough that the Node project stopped treating Alpine builds as fully tier-1 supported. Benchmarks vary, and the honest statement is: test your own workload rather than assume either way.
  • Default thread stack size is much smaller (128 KB versus glibc's 8 MB), which can produce stack overflows in code that is fine elsewhere.

The practical recommendation for Node images, stated plainly: node:20-slim (Debian-based, glibc, around 75 MB) is the safer default; node:20-alpine is worth it when image size genuinely matters and you have tested your dependency tree on it. A distroless image (Google's gcr.io/distroless/nodejs20) is another option — glibc, no shell, no package manager, minimal attack surface — with the trade that you cannot exec into it and must debug with ephemeral containers (Node 28).

Static linking's return

Go statically links by default and produces a binary depending on nothing but the kernel's syscall ABI (Node 4). Hence FROM scratch images of a few megabytes containing exactly one file. Rust with the musl target does the same. Node cannot — it is a large C++ program with a dynamic-linking-dependent addon system — though single-executable applications (Node 20+) bundle your JavaScript into the binary, which is a related but different idea.

Where this is in your stack

Node's module system is dynamic linking, one level up, and the parallel continues from Node 33. require() at run time resolves a name to a file, loads it, executes it, caches it. That is: symbol lookup, mapping, initialisation, and a load cache — the dynamic linker's job. The module cache (require.cache) is why a module executes once no matter how many times it is required, which is exactly why a shared object's initialisation runs once. Dynamic import() is lazy loading, and the bundler's code-split chunks are shared objects fetched on demand. And node_modules's nested resolution is the reason two versions of a package can coexist in one program — something the ELF dynamic linker cannot easily do, and a genuine advantage of npm's design that people rarely credit.

The multi-stage build for a Node service, with every line explained by this part:

FROM node:20-slim AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci                    # native addons compiled here (Node 33),
                              # against this image's glibc
COPY . .
RUN npm run build

FROM node:20-slim             # same base ⇒ same libc ⇒ addons still load
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci --omit=dev         # no compiler, no dev deps in the final image
COPY --from=build /app/dist ./dist
USER node                     # Node 25
CMD ["node", "dist/server.js"]  # exec form ⇒ node is PID 1 ⇒ signals work (Node 30)

Copying node_modules from the host into an image is broken for two independent reasons from this part: the native addons were compiled for your host's architecture (Node 33) and against your host's libc (this node). Both fail at run time with errors that do not obviously say so — invalid ELF header for the first, cannot open shared object file or a symbol version error for the second. .dockerignore containing node_modules is not a size optimisation; it is a correctness requirement.

What you can do at this point: explain dynamic linking including PLT/GOT and lazy binding, use ldd and LD_PRELOAD, explain glibc's compatibility asymmetry and derive the build-on-the-oldest rule, state the real trade-offs of Alpine versus slim versus distroless with reasons rather than preferences, and explain why host node_modules must never be copied into an image.

What's still broken: the file is complete and loadable, and nothing has said what happens when you actually run it — what execve does to the calling process, how the shell turns a command line into a process tree, or how npm start becomes five processes.

Node 35 — execve, and how a command becomes a process tree

The limitation: a complete, loadable executable exists on disk and nothing has said what running it does.

What execve does

execve(path, argv, envp) replaces the program running in the current process. It does not create a process. The PID stays the same, the parent stays the same, open file descriptors stay open, credentials stay as they are — but the address space is destroyed and rebuilt.

In order:

  1. Permission check — is the file executable by this process? Is it setuid, and if so whose identity does the new program take (Node 25)?
  2. Read the header. If it starts with #!, this is a script: read the interpreter path from that first line, and re-run execve on the interpreter with the script's path as an argument. This is the shebang, and it is handled by the kernel rather than the shell. Two details bite: the interpreter path must be absolute, which is why #!/usr/bin/env node is used — env is at a known location and finds node on PATH; and only one argument is portably allowed after the interpreter.
  3. Tear down the old address space. Every mapping from Node 11 is discarded. This is why fork-then-exec copies essentially nothing (Node 14) — the copy-on-write pages are dropped before any are written.
  4. Map the new program's segments from its ELF program headers (Node 33), demand-paged so almost nothing is read yet (Node 13).
  5. Build a new stack containing argv, envp, and the auxiliary vector — kernel-supplied values like the page size, the vDSO address (Node 8), and the ASLR seed.
  6. Close descriptors marked close-on-exec (Node 22); everything else survives, which is what makes redirection work.
  7. Reset signal handlers to default — the handler code no longer exists. The signal mask, however, is inherited, which is a genuine trap: a process that blocked a signal and then execs passes that block to the new program, and there are real bugs where a service never receives SIGTERM because something up the chain blocked it.
  8. Jump to the interpreter named in PT_INTERP — the dynamic linker (Node 34) — which maps the shared libraries, performs relocations, runs initialisers, and finally jumps to the program's entry point.
  9. The C runtime sets up its own state and calls main.

execve only returns on failure. If it succeeds there is nothing to return to, because the code that called it no longer exists.

The environment

envp is a plain array of KEY=VALUE strings copied onto the new stack. That is all an environment variable is: per-process memory, inherited by copy at exec, with no kernel involvement after that. [EXPLAIN] Everything about their behaviour follows:

  • A child cannot modify its parent's environment. There is no channel to do so.
  • Changing a variable after a process starts does not affect it — hence restarting a service after editing its configuration.
  • process.env.FOO = 'x' in Node modifies your copy and affects only children spawned afterwards.
  • They are visible. /proc/<pid>/environ exposes them to anyone who can read that file, and ps eww shows them. This is why secrets in environment variables are a compromise rather than a best practice — they leak into crash dumps, error reporting payloads, subprocess environments, and CI logs. The 12-factor recommendation is about configuration; for secrets, mounted files with restrictive permissions (Kubernetes secret volumes, and the reason _FILE-suffixed variables are a common convention) are meaningfully better.
  • PATH is searched by the shell or by execvp, not by execve — the bare syscall requires a full path. This is why child_process.spawn('node') can fail with ENOENT in an environment with a minimal PATH, and why ENOENT from spawn means "the program was not found", not "your file argument was not found", which misleads people every time.

What the shell does with one command line

Typing ls -l | grep .js > out.txt produces, in order: tokenising and expansion (globs, variables, command substitution, all done by the shell — which is why rm *.txt is expanded before rm ever sees it, and why a filename containing a space breaks unquoted scripts); creating a pipe; forking twice; wiring descriptors with dup2 in each child (Node 22); execve in each; and waitpid in the parent to collect statuses.

Also worth knowing: a shell puts each pipeline in its own process group, and the terminal has a foreground group. Ctrl-C sends SIGINT to every process in the foreground group, which is why it interrupts a whole pipeline. Backgrounding with & means the group is not the foreground one, and a background process that reads from the terminal receives SIGTTIN and stops.

Where this is in your stack

npm start is a process tree, and knowing its shape explains a family of bugs. A typical chain is: your shell → npm (a Node process) → sh -c "node server.js" → node server.js. Four processes, three fork/exec pairs. The consequences:

  • Signals do not reach the bottom. SIGTERM to npm is not automatically forwarded through the shell to your server. In a container this is Node 30's PID 1 problem compounded — which is why the correct production entrypoint is CMD ["node", "server.js"], not CMD ["npm", "start"]. Running npm in production containers costs you signal handling, a process, memory, and startup time, for no benefit.
  • Exit codes are relayed, sometimes badly, and npm adds its own error output on a non-zero exit which obscures the real one.
  • node_modules/.bin is prepended to PATH by npm before running a script — which is the entire mechanism by which "test": "jest" finds jest, and it is PATH plus symbolic links (Node 21) plus a shebang, nothing more.

exec in a shell script is this system call exposed. An entrypoint script that ends with exec node server.js replaces the shell with Node, so Node becomes PID 1 and receives signals directly. Without exec, the shell stays as PID 1 with Node as its child, and shutdown breaks. This single word is the difference between a container that shuts down gracefully and one that does not, and it is the standard fix for entrypoint scripts that need to do setup before starting the app.

spawn versus exec in Node. child_process.spawn is fork+execve directly, streaming output. child_process.exec runs the command through a shell and buffers the output — so it supports pipes and globs, and it is a command injection vulnerability if any part of the string comes from user input, because the shell will interpret ;, &&, backticks and $(). Use spawn with an argument array, where each argument is passed as a separate argv entry and no shell is involved. Also recall Node 22: exec's maxBuffer deadlock on chatty children.

The setuid check in step 1 explains a Docker security control. allowPrivilegeEscalation: false in a Kubernetes security context sets the no_new_privs bit, which makes the kernel ignore setuid bits at execve. So even if a setuid-root binary exists in the image, executing it grants nothing. It is a one-line, near-zero-cost mitigation for a whole class of container escape, and it operates precisely at step 1 of this node.

What you can do at this point: describe execve step by step including the shebang and what survives it, explain environment variables as inherited stack memory and derive every consequence including why they are poor secret storage, explain what a shell does with a command line and what process groups are for, explain npm start's process tree and why exec in an entrypoint matters, and choose correctly between spawn and exec on security grounds.

What's still broken: every process so far has a parent. Following the chain upward reaches PID 1, and PID 1 has no parent — it was started by the kernel. But the kernel is itself a program in a file, so something had to load it, on a machine where nothing is running at all.

Node 36 — boot: from no electricity to PID 1

The limitation: every process has a parent, the chain ends at PID 1, and PID 1 was started by the kernel — which is itself a program in a file that something had to load, on a machine where nothing is running.

The bootstrap problem, stated exactly: to load a program you need a program. The resolution is that the first program is not loaded at all — it is physically present in non-volatile memory, at an address the processor is wired to start from.

Power-on

When power is applied, the processor's registers are set to hardware-defined values. On x86 the instruction pointer starts at 0xFFFFFFF0, sixteen bytes below the top of the address space, and the chipset arranges for the firmware flash chip on the motherboard to be visible there. So the first instruction executed by any x86 machine is a jump into firmware, and that fact is fixed in silicon. Before even that, on any modern machine, other processors have already run: Intel's Management Engine or AMD's Platform Security Processor initialises and, on secure-boot systems, verifies the firmware before the main processor is released.

Firmware: BIOS then UEFI

BIOS — Basic Input/Output System, from the original IBM PC in 1981 — runs a power-on self test, initialises the memory controller and basic devices, and then does something remarkably crude: reads the first 512-byte sector of the boot device into memory at 0x7C00, checks that its last two bytes are 0x55 0xAA, and jumps to it. That sector is the Master Boot Record. It has 512 bytes total, of which 64 are the partition table and 2 are the signature — leaving 446 bytes for code, which is not enough to do anything except load a larger loader. Hence multi-stage bootloaders. BIOS also starts the processor in 16-bit real mode, compatible with the 1978 8086, so an operating system's first job is to switch modes.

UEFI replaced it and is what any machine built in the last decade uses. It is a small operating system in its own right: it understands the FAT32 filesystem, so it can read files from an EFI System Partition rather than raw sectors; it starts the processor in 32- or 64-bit mode; it has a boot manager with a variable store (efibootmgr edits it); and it supports Secure Boot, verifying each stage's cryptographic signature before executing it. That chain is why an unsigned kernel module fails to load on a Secure Boot system, and why installing a proprietary GPU driver requires enrolling a key or disabling the feature.

The bootloader

GRUB2 on most Linux systems, systemd-boot on some, and cloud images often use a minimal one. Its job: present a choice of kernels, then load the kernel image and the initramfs into memory, assemble the kernel command line, and jump to the kernel's entry point.

The kernel command line is worth knowing because it is where several things you configure end up: root=UUID=... (which filesystem to mount as /), ro, quiet, and options like systemd.unified_cgroup_hierarchy=1 (Node 27), transparent_hugepage=never (Node 10), nvme_core.io_timeout, or mitigations=off (Node 40 — a real performance choice with a real security cost). It is visible at run time in /proc/cmdline.

The kernel starts

The kernel image is compressed with a small decompressor stub at the front, which unpacks the real kernel into memory. Then, in rough order: set up early page tables and switch to virtual addressing (Node 10); initialise the interrupt descriptor table (Node 2); detect processors and bring the secondary cores out of halt; initialise the memory allocator, the scheduler (Node 7), and the timers (Node 8); initialise built-in drivers; and mount the initramfs.

The initramfs deserves an explanation because its existence is a chicken-and-egg fix. The kernel must mount the root filesystem, which requires a driver for the storage controller and for the filesystem — but those may be modules living on that filesystem. So the bootloader loads a small compressed archive into memory, the kernel unpacks it as a temporary root, and it contains exactly the modules and tools needed to find and mount the real root: storage drivers, LVM, disk encryption (this is where the passphrase prompt comes from), and network filesystem support. Once the real root is mounted, the initramfs is discarded.

Finally the kernel executes /sbin/init as PID 1 — its only exec, and the first user-mode process on the machine. Every process that will ever run descends from it (Node 5). If it cannot be started, the kernel panics with Kernel panic - not syncing: No working init found, which is what a broken initrd or a wrong root= parameter produces.

Where this is in your stack

A container has no boot, and that is the whole reason it starts in milliseconds. [EXPLAIN] Nothing in this node happens: no firmware, no bootloader, no kernel decompression, no device initialisation, no initramfs, no init system. Starting a container is clone() plus execve() (Node 28) on a kernel that is already running. A virtual machine, by contrast, performs this entire sequence — virtual firmware, virtual bootloader, a full kernel boot — which is why it takes seconds and why Firecracker's 125-millisecond boot (Node 29) is an engineering achievement: it is a stripped virtual machine monitor that skips almost all of the legacy sequence, uses a minimal kernel with direct boot, and emulates five devices.

Cloud instance startup is this node plus a configuration phase. An EC2 instance boots the sequence above, then cloud-init runs as an early service: it fetches instance metadata from the link-local address 169.254.169.254, sets the hostname, installs SSH keys, grows the root partition to the volume size, and runs your user-data script. Understanding that it is an ordinary service explains why user-data runs once by default, why its logs are in /var/log/cloud-init-output.log, and why the metadata endpoint being reachable from your application is a security concern — SSRF against it was the mechanism of the 2019 Capital One breach, and IMDSv2's session-token requirement exists to make that harder.

The kernel is the one thing containers do not bring with them, which follows from Node 26 and lands here concretely. Your Alpine container running on an Ubuntu host uses the Ubuntu kernel. So a container cannot require a newer kernel feature than the host provides, and features like cgroups v2, io_uring, and newer seccomp actions depend on the host. This is why "it works on my machine" can be a kernel version difference, and why managed Kubernetes providers publish node image versions as a meaningful thing to track.

Immutable infrastructure is a statement about this node. Rather than booting a machine and configuring it, you build an image containing the configuration and boot that. The boot sequence is unchanged; what changes is that no step after it modifies the system. Container images are the same principle, one layer up, without the boot.

What you can do at this point: explain the bootstrap problem and how the first instruction is reached, describe the BIOS and UEFI paths and what Secure Boot verifies, explain what the initramfs is for, describe kernel startup through to PID 1, explain precisely why containers start in milliseconds and virtual machines do not, and explain cloud-init and the instance metadata service.

What's still broken: PID 1 is running and the machine has exactly one process. Something must now start every service, in the right order, with the right dependencies, restarting them when they fail — and the design of that program was the most divisive argument in Linux for a decade.

Node 37 — init, systemd, and what a "service" is

The limitation: PID 1 is running and the machine has exactly one process. Something must start every service, in the right order, and keep it running.

What PID 1 has to do

Four jobs, and every one of them recurs in your orchestration tooling:

  1. Start services, respecting dependencies — the network before anything that binds a port, the database before the application.
  2. Supervise them — notice when one dies and decide whether to restart it, with a backoff so a crash loop does not consume the machine.
  3. Reap orphans (Node 5). PID 1 inherits every process whose parent died and must collect their exit statuses or the PID space leaks.
  4. Shut down cleanly — signal everything, in dependency order, with a deadline (Node 30).

SysV init, and why it was replaced

The traditional design: /etc/inittab named a runlevel (a number: 3 for multi-user text, 5 for graphical, 0 halt, 6 reboot), and each runlevel had a directory of shell scripts run in filename order — S10network, S20ssh, S99myapp. Each script accepted start, stop, restart and status.

Its problems were structural rather than cosmetic:

  • Strictly sequential. Ordering was expressed by numbering, so nothing could start in parallel even when independent. Boot took as long as the sum of every service.
  • No real dependency model. Numbers encode order, not requirement. If a service needed the network, the best you could do was pick a higher number and hope.
  • No supervision. Once a script forked a daemon and exited, init had no relationship with it. A crashed service stayed dead. Every project therefore invented its own supervisor — daemontools, runit, supervisord, monit, and in the Node world forever and pm2.
  • Tracking processes was guesswork. A "daemonised" process double-forks to detach from its parent, so init loses track of it entirely. Scripts wrote PID files, which go stale, which is why service foo status so often lied.

systemd

Written by Lennart Poettering and Kay Sievers at Red Hat, first released in 2010 and adopted by essentially every major distribution between 2012 and 2015 — over sustained and often bitter objection, on the grounds that it was doing far more than an init system should. Whatever one's view of that argument, it is what runs on the servers you deploy to, and its design decisions are worth understanding because they solve the four problems above directly.

Units, declared not scripted. A unit file is configuration rather than code. Types include .service (a process), .socket, .timer (a cron replacement), .mount, .target (a grouping, replacing runlevels), and .path.

Real dependencies, and ordering separated from requirement. Requires= states that another unit must be active; After= states ordering.
